MOTIONS 

The number, variety and types of motions which occur in legal proceedings are as extensive as the proceedings themselves. There are motions to strike, motions to vacate, motions for leave to amend, and motions, in short, for any order which it is desired to have the court make. An application for an order is denominated a MOTION (sometimes an order to show cause), and every direction of a court or judge entered in writing and not included in a judgment, is denominated an 

ORDER. (CCP 1003) 

Motions are made in the court in which the action is pending and unless made during the course of a trial, they ordinarily must be upon written notice (5 days if in the county in which an attorney for the party notified has his office, otherwise 10 days) (CCP 1005)

If on motion a sum of money is ordered paid, it may be enforced by execution in the same manner as by a judgment. (CCP 1007)

If a motion has been made and refused, in whole or in part, or granted conditionally, or on terms, and it is desired to present a subsequent motion for the same order, it must be shown by affidavit what application was before made, when and to what judge, what order was made and what new facts are claimed to be shown as justifying a new order. A violation of this requirement may be punished as a contempt and the order may be vacated ex parte. (CCP 1008) NOTICES

General Rule-Notice of motion must state: 

1. When (date and hour) to be made 

2. Grounds (legal basis) 

3. The papers, if any (factual basis) 

If any paper mentioned in the notice has not previously been served upon the party to be

notified and was not filed by him, a copy thereof must accompany the notice. (CCP 1010) 

4. Points and authorities

The rules for service of the notice are as prescribed in 

CCP 1011, 1012, 1013, 1015. (For certain specific motions, see particular subject)

Procedure-same as MOTION TO STRIKE, Procedure

(In Superior Courts by Judicial Council Rule and in some Municipal Courts by local rule, a memorandum of points and authorities relied upon must accompany or be endorsed on every demurrer and every notice of motion other than a notice of intention to move for new trial), (See Judicial Council rules of the Superior Court, Rule XIX, subdivision 1, and local Municipal Court rules) Explanation

MOTION TO STRIKE 

(CCP 453)

A motion to strike is the procedure by which a party moves to have the court delete a part or the whole of a pleading claimed to be sham, irrelevant or redundant. 

Upon such motion duly noticed, the court may, upon such conditions as appear just, strike out the improper matter.

The notice of motion to strike is frequently filed at the same time as a demurrer and often accompanies or precedes a motion for judgment on the pleadings.

The notice of motion to strike is not a pleading (see 

CCP 422) and does not prevent the entry of a default for failure to answer or demur within the time prescribed in the summons (or fixed by the court). Procedure 

1. Prepare notice of motion to strike †1 original to file 

1 to serve on opposing counsel 

2. Serve copy on opposing counsel 

3. File original with proof of service 

If there has been no prior appearance of the defendant as by demurrer or answer, the usual appearance fee must be paid

4774 4. See court rules regarding hearing (or consult law and motion department). In some counties motions are heard only on prescribed days 5. After hearing, unless waived, prevailing party must serve on adverse party notice of ruling on motion

NOTICE TO PRODUCE (CCP 1938)

If a writing in the custody of the adverse party is desired for use at a trial, he must first have reasonable notice to produce it. If he fails to do so secondary evidence as to the contents may be introduced. Procedure Prepare †1 original to file with proof of service

To Associate New Attorney

When a new attorney comes into a case, even though he does not replace a prior attorney, he may properly become an attorney of record ONLY by an order of substitution. (No method is provided other than that of CCP 284) (ex.) If attorney A wishes to become associated Explanation with attorney B, the proper motion is that attorneys A and B be substituted as counsel for plaintiff (defendant) in the place and stead of attorney A; or this may be accomplished by a written substitution in the form set forth above.

VERIFICATIONS 

A verification is a sworn statement as to the truth of the matters alleged in a pleading, and is not necessary except where expressly required by statute. However, most attorneys consider it advisable and good practice to have ALL pleadings verified. 

There are many instances in which verification is either mandatory or desirable. In addition to those specifically mentioned herein, consult the statutory provisions as to each document filed. 

1. REQUIRED (most important instances) 

Complaint or Petition 

(a) Action within Class B Justice's Court jurisdiction, no matter where filed, unless separate affidavit or waiver is filed or entered as provided in CCP 396a (b) Injunction (CCP 527) (c) Writ of Review (CCP 1069)

Writ of Mandamus (CCP 1086) 

Writ of Prohibition (CCP 1103)

(d) Unlawful Detainer (CCP 1166) 

(e) Quiet Title (CCP 749) (f) To Establish Fact of Death (PR C 1170) 

(g) Cross complaint in divorce action (personal verification required) (CC 128) (h) For involuntary dissolution of corporation (CC 404, 404a) (i) Civil actions in Police Courts (CCP 122)

Note: 

Answer 

Complaint of state, county, or officer of either, need not be verified (a) When complaint is verified, answer must be (CCP 437) may be though complaint is not (b) When state, city or county, or officer thereof, is PLAINTIFF (not if defendant) (c) Not required where it would subject to criminal prosecution

Note: Genuineness and execution of instrument pleaded in complaint are deemed admitted unless answer denying same is verified (CCP 447) If pleaded in answer-deemed admitted unless affidavit denying same is served and filed within 10 days (CCP 448) (see exception in CCP 449)

Cost Bill (CCP 1033-4) 

Claim-Mechanic's Lien (CCP 1184, 1187)

Bill of Particulars 

If pleading is verified bill of particulars must be verified (CCP 454)

2. To be MADE BY (CCP 446) 

Forms (a) Plaintiff, defendant, petitioner (b) One of parties for all or several (c) Attorney, if party is outside of county or unable to verify (d) Authorized officer of corporation (e) Member of a copartnership (f) Agent Usually printed on cover to be filled in-if not on cover or to be filed in county that does not use covers, type immediately following pleading

XIX 

AIDS TO LEGAL SECRETARIES 

Suggestions in General from Judges, Clerks and Attorneys 1. Double space and indent all descriptions of property and long legal quotations. (Some courts require it, but all judges and clerks appreciate it.) 

2. Always use available printed forms, unless a great deal of interlineation is required, to facilitate work of court. 

3. Use full title of document on pleadings, such as "Answer to Complaint," "Answer to Cross Complaint," "Affidavit for 

99 etc. 

4. Do not use SUPRA, but repeat entire citation unless it has been cited on same page or is referred to many times. In the latter case say in the "Jones case," "Smith case,”. 

5. Remember, first impressions as to the character of an office may be formed on the character of its secretarial work. Neatness and accuracy are good recommendations.

Striking a wrong key is easily understandable, but permitting the document to leave the office with a typographical error is not excusable. 

6. Change carbon paper sufficiently often to assure clear, legible copies. Do not run too many copies at a time. 

7. Be certain correct number is on document. 

8. Spell names correctly and double check property descriptions. 

9. Be certain that documents requiring notarization contain seal and signature of notary.

10. Be certain that document bears all required signatures. 

11. During dictation if a point is not clear, and you do not understand the content of the dictation, ask questions. Assume nothing. When in doubt, ASK! Mistakes are costly. 

12. Conform all copies of documents to originals re signatures, dates, notaries, hours, departments, names, numbers, etc. 

13. Use exact size of paper, and follow all other current rules of a particular court. 

14. Assemble like carbon copies together instead of assembling them indiscriminately with No. 1 copies and No. 3's etc. mixed together; otherwise the resulting conglomeration of improperly assembled papers is far from easy to read and is very untidy. 

15. Call the clerk of the court in which a document is to be filed, when in doubt. This obviates needless work by the secretary and hard feelings against the clerk, but do not ask clerk or sheriff's deputy to practice law for your employer. 

16. Check legal paper and note on calendar time and place of hearing for matters concerning your office. 

17. Docket all proceedings immediately. 

18. Do not end a document so that only signatures appear on last page. 

19. Always number first and last pages of every document as well as those between. 

20. DO NOT answer technical questions of clients which amount to "legal advice," or fill in legal forms, unless properly instructed by your employer. 

You might easily be mistaken and besides that is the attorney's job.

AIDS TO LEGAL SECRETARIES 

471 

21. Of course, you will not let anyone pump you about office business-nor spill it without pumping; even the fact that a particular person is a client is a professional secret. 

22. See GENERAL PROVISIONS AND SUGGES- 

TIONS under PROBATE, p 269, many are applicable to general procedure for all courts.

Witnesseth 

Know all Men by these Presents

TYPOGRAPICAL AIDS 

In making brackets down the middle of page in caption of a document set the shift lock for capitals, turn the carriage with the left hand, hold down space bar with right thumb and strike the parenthesis with the middle finger.

When making an exact copy of an instrument which contains a typographical error, indicate error by underlining it, such as: Grand Deed.

When making an erasure, insert card (not paper) immediately behind original and before the carbon.

To insert page of a stapled document without unstapling, roll blank sheet of paper in typewriter to extend an inch or so above platen. Slip page to be changed between

platen and extended sheet; roll platen back and make change.

Ask stationer to cut corners (upper left and lower right) of carbon paper, and order paper an inch longer to facilitate removal of a number of pages. 

TIME SAVERS 

Much time can be saved in retyping agreements and long instruments, by writing them on letter size, plain paper. 

Write corporation articles, by-laws, application, etc. on letter size paper and make minute book copies at same time as original.

Many lawyers now avoid use of "party of the first part," "first party," and needless repetition of full names in agreements, preferring to use "husband" and "wife," "Corporation," "Smith," "Jones," etc., as being less confusing and saving time. 

See "fictitious names and capacities" paragraph (b) on page 29.

YOU ARE YOUR EMPLOYER'S PRINCIPAL TIME SAVER—THINK AHEAD, LIGHTEN HIS WORK, SAVE HIM FROM TEDIOUS DETAILS. 

HE CAN THEN EARN MORE MONEY, BE LESS FATIGUED AND HE MAY GIVE YOU A 

RAISE!

AIDS TO LEGAL SECRETARIES 

473 

PITFALLS AND MONEY SAVERS 

1. Don't

Prepare papers on an appeal on the judgment roll (without a bill of exceptions) and expect your employer to be able to argue the case on the merits to any appellate court. If relying on the judgment roll alone BE SURE to know what it will and will not contain. (CCP 670, 1138, 1139) ex. On an appeal from an order denying or 

2. Don't granting a motion the affidavits and other evidence used on the motion are NOT contained in the judgment roll; the only way to have the appellate court consider such affidavits (or other evidence) is to make up a bill of exceptions and set them out (CCP 

951, 952; 2 Cal Jur 601, et seq. and cases cited; or in appeals from the municipal court, if the papers are on file in that court refer to them and index and identify them as required by CCP 988a; see also Morgan v. 

Neff, 140 CA 757) 

Prepare a Bill of Exceptions without including specifications of the particulars wherein the evidence is insufficient if its sufficiency to support any finding, express or implied, is to be challenged (CCP 

648; Liveoak Cemetery Assn. v. Adamson (1930) 

106 C A 783; Loewenberg v. Schneider (1939) 14 

Cal (2d) 305, 307)

The purpose of the requirement for such specifications is to direct the attention of the opponent and

trial court thereto in order that if such evidence has been produced at the trial and omitted from the proposed bill of exceptions, the deficiency can be cured before engrossment of the final bill. Without the specification the appellate court will presume that the evidence was adduced and was omitted because its sufficiency was not challenged. 

3. Don't

Fail to make for your form file an extra copy of any legal form or jury instruction on a proposition of law not already in your form file or this book. It may save time and effort in some other case-and don't fail to index it. 

4. Don't

Use sarcasm in a brief unless your employer insists (it is the least persuasive form of argumentation) or let him forget that a brief should be BRIEF. 

5. Don't

When the judgment is prepared and entered by the clerk, as in the municipal court, fail to promptly get a copy for your employer or note on the calendar for him to inspect the original. Many instances have been observed (and many appeals taken) where a judgment purportedly for the defendant was invalid because it was drawn solely as a judgment that he recover costs without adjudicating that plaintiff take nothing on the complaint or otherwise determining the action. An award of costs is invalid except as an incident to an otherwise valid judgment.
